<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — How Claude works

```text
Here is a question that will save you hours. When Claude gets something wrong, is it the model, or is it you? By the end of this lecture you will be able to answer that in seconds, and you will know exactly which Claude model to reach for, for any task, without guessing or overspending.
```

Scene 2 — Why this matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because two expensive mistakes hide here. The first is overspending, sending simple, repetitive jobs to the most powerful model and burning through your limits or your budget. The second is underpowering, asking a fast model to untangle a complex decision and then trusting a shallow answer. Think of it like hiring. You would not ask your most senior strategist to rename five hundred files, and you would not ask a new intern to restructure your pricing. The skill is matching the job to the right level of expertise, and, just as importantly, diagnosing why an answer was weak before you decide what to change.
```

Scene 3 — Two sources of knowledge

```text
Every answer Claude gives comes from exactly two places. The first is training, everything the model learned before a cut-off date. The second is the context window, which is everything in this conversation right now. Your messages, the files you attach, your preferences, remembered details if memory is switched on, and anything fetched by tools like web search or a connector to your Google Drive. If a fact is in neither place, Claude cannot know it. It may still produce a confident sentence, and that is exactly why we check.
```

Scene 4 — Three classic surprises

```text
That one idea explains the three most common complaints. Out of date answers? Training has a cut-off, so turn on web search or give Claude the source. Generic answers? The context is thin, so add your audience, your goal and an example of good work. Muddled answers in a very long chat? The context is full of old detours. Start a fresh chat and paste a five line summary of what matters. Notice that none of these fixes involve changing the model.
```

Scene 5 — The model families

```text
Now the models. Anthropic names its tiers, and the pattern has been stable even as version numbers change. Haiku is the fast, low cost tier for high volume work like tagging or extraction. Sonnet is the balanced everyday workhorse. Opus is the most capable mainstream tier, built for hard reasoning, complex coding and long agent tasks. And in twenty twenty six Anthropic added Fable, its most capable widely released tier, priced above Opus. As of September twenty twenty six the line up includes Claude Haiku four point five, Sonnet five, Opus five point five and Fable five point one. Those numbers will move. The ladder will not.
```

Scene 6 — Thinking and effort

```text
Newer Claude models can also think before they answer. In the apps you will see an extended thinking option. For developers it is an effort setting, from low up to max. Think of effort like asking a colleague for a quick opinion versus asking them to go away and work it through properly. For a caption rewrite, the quick opinion is fine. For a pricing model with twelve constraints, you want the worked version, and you are happy to wait for it.
```

Scene 7 — The four-question rule

```text
Here is the rule I want you to remember. Is the task simple, repetitive or high volume? Start fast. Is it everyday knowledge work, drafting, summarising, most coding? Use the balanced default. Is it complex, high stakes or long running? Go to the most capable model and switch thinking on. And if the answer is still weak, fix the prompt before you upgrade anything. In practice, most bad answers are context problems wearing a model costume.
```

Scene 8 — One agency, three tiers

```text
Picture a twelve person social agency in Dubai. Every week it tags five thousand comments for sentiment, and that runs on a fast tier through the API with a fixed label list and a human spot check of fifty comments. Weekly client reports are drafted by the balanced model inside a client Project, then edited by an account manager. Once a quarter the strategist reviews pricing, weighing retainers, freelancer costs and churn risk, and she uses the most capable model with thinking on, asking it to list every assumption so she can challenge them. Same company, three different choices, each one deliberate.
```

Scene 9 — Common traps

```text
Three traps to avoid. First, assuming the biggest model is always best. It is slower and it uses up your limits faster, and for a simple rewrite it rarely changes the result. Second, trusting model names from an old blog post or video. Tiers get renamed and retired, so the model picker and Anthropic's own documentation are your source of truth. Third, blaming the model for missing context. If the answer does not mention your pricing, it is almost always because nobody gave Claude your pricing. You will know you are choosing well when your default model handles most tasks without rework, and you move up a tier deliberately, not out of habit.
```

Scene 10 — Simple example

```text
Let's start with a simple example. Take one caption, say, new autumn menu launches Friday, come early for the pumpkin latte, and ask a fast model and the most capable model to make it friendlier. The two answers will be almost identical, and the fast one arrives first. Now take a harder task. Compare three delivery pricing options for a small bakery, with fuel costs, minimum order values and a target margin. Here the gap opens up. The more capable model, with thinking on, lays out a table, spots that one option loses money on small orders, and lists its assumptions. The fast model gives a tidy paragraph that misses the loss. Same person, same afternoon, two different right answers about which model to use.
```

Scene 11 — Try this now

```text
Try this now. Pause the video and pick one task you do every week, a report summary, a client email, a data check. Open two fresh chats. In the first, choose a fast or balanced model. In the second, the most capable model with thinking on. Paste exactly the same prompt and the same attachment into both. Then score each answer from one to five for quality, and write down how many minutes it would take you to make it usable. If the scores are close, the faster model wins for that task. If the capable model saves you ten minutes of fixing, it wins. Either way, you now have evidence, not a hunch, and it took less than fifteen minutes.
```

Scene 12 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me show you exactly how I run this comparison. I open two fresh chats side by side. In the left one I open the model picker and choose a fast model. In the right one I choose the most capable model and switch extended thinking on. Now I paste the comparison prompt from the lesson into both. Context, I am the operations lead at a bakery chain in Leeds and this goes to our finance director. Task, compare the three delivery options in the attached quote. Constraints, use only the attached material, say not in the material when something is missing, under two hundred words. Format, a table then a one line recommendation. Then I attach exactly the same PDF quote to both chats and press send in each.
```

Scene 13 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Now I read both answers the way a finance director would. The fast answer is tidy, but it ignores the surcharge for orders under fifty pounds, so I mark it amber. The capable model builds a table, flags that option two loses money on small orders, and lists its assumptions. I score them. Quality, three for the fast answer, five for the capable one. Minutes to make it usable, about twelve versus three. For this task, the capable model wins, and I write that in my model guide. Then I run a second task, rewriting a product caption, and the scores come out equal, so the fast model wins that one. Two tasks, two evidence based decisions, in about fifteen minutes.
```

Scene 14 — Your next step

```text
So, to recap. Claude knows only its training plus what is in the context. The model families run from fast to most capable, and names change, so trust the picker, not old blog posts. Use the four question rule, and fix prompts before you upgrade. Your next step is in the lesson text: a comparison prompt you run on two models with the same real task. Score both, keep the table, and re-run it whenever a new model arrives. That table becomes your personal, evidence based model guide.
```

</details>

### 2. Chat fundamentals: the prompt structure experts use

Lesson `chat-fundamentals-and-prompt-structure` · module “Claude foundations” · target 9 min · 15 scenes · 1310 words · planned 9:30 (words ≈ 9:30) · status: **to produce**

| # | Time | Sec | Narration | On screen | Visual |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0:00 | 28 | Most people type a request into Claude the way they would type into a search box. Five words, fingers crossed. Experts do something different, and it takes about thirty seconds. In this lecture you will learn the four part structure professionals use, how to keep pasted material from getting mixed up, and how to iterate like an editor instead of pulling a slot machine lever. | Prompts that work<br>• Context<br>• Task<br>• Constraints<br>• Format | Layout: full-screen slide. Title card showing four stacked blocks labelled Context, Task, Constraints, Format snapping together like building bricks. Timing (28s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-17s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~22s; hold to 28s. |
| 2 | 0:28 | 43 | Why does structure matter so much? Because Claude cannot see inside your head. When you type write a post about our new service, you know the audience, the tone, the length and what your competitors are saying. Claude knows none of it. Every missing detail becomes a guess, and guesses average out to bland. Here is an analogy. Ordering from a tailor, you would not say make me a suit. You would give measurements, fabric, occasion and a deadline. A prompt is a set of measurements. The four part structure simply makes sure you never forget the important ones. | Why structure matters<br>• Claude cannot read your mind<br>• Every missing detail becomes a guess | Layout: full-screen slide. Slide shows a thought bubble full of details (audience, tone, length) above a person; a thin arrow carries only three words into a chat box. At 5s the missing details drop out of the bubble as grey question marks. Timing (43s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-26s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~34s; hold to 43s. |
| 3 | 1:11 | 36 | Imagine briefing a talented new hire on their first day. You would never just say, write a post. You would say who it is for, what it needs to achieve, what the rules are and what the finished thing should look like. That is exactly the structure. Context, task, constraints and format. Claude's newer models follow instructions very precisely, so be direct. If you want suggestions only, say suggest, do not rewrite. If you want changes made, say rewrite. Precision in, precision out. | Brief it like a new colleague<br>• Who is it for?<br>• What do you want?<br>• What are the rules?<br>• What does done look like? | Layout: animated scene. A manager handing a clearly labelled folder to a new team member, the folder tabs reading audience, goal, rules, format. Timing (36s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-22s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~29s; hold to 36s. |
| 4 | 1:47 | 25 | Context does the heaviest lifting, and the most overlooked part of context is why. Telling Claude that procurement managers skim on their phones changes sentence length, structure and tone, all at once, without you writing three extra rules. When you explain the reason behind a constraint, the model can generalise it sensibly to situations you did not anticipate. | Context is the multiplier<br>• Audience<br>• Situation<br>• Why it matters | Layout: full-screen slide. Before and after comparison of two short prompts; the second gains a highlighted context sentence and the resulting output grows noticeably more specific. Timing (25s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-15s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~20s; hold to 25s. |
| 5 | 2:12 | 30 | Next, tags. When you paste a brief, brand rules and three past posts into one message, wrap each in a labelled tag, such as brief, brand rules, past posts, written inside angle brackets. Claude is trained to respect that structure, so it stops blending details between documents. Even better, you can refer back precisely. Check every caption against the brand rules. It is a small habit with an outsized effect. | Tags keep materials apart<br>• brief<br>• brand rules<br>• past posts | Layout: full-screen slide. Code-style screen showing text wrapped in angle-bracket tags, each tag glowing a different colour, then an instruction referencing one tag by name. Timing (30s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-18s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~24s; hold to 30s. |
| 6 | 2:42 | 26 | The strongest steering tool you have is an example. One or two short samples of what good looks like will beat a paragraph of adjectives like punchy or on brand. Just be explicit about what to copy, such as the structure, length and tone, and what not to copy, such as the specific product names or facts in the example. | Examples beat adjectives<br>• Show 1 to 2 samples<br>• Say what to copy and what not to | Layout: full-screen slide. Two example captions slide in labelled Example; arrows highlight tone and structure while a crossed-out label marks specific facts as do not copy. Timing (26s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-16s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~21s; hold to 26s. |
| 7 | 3:08 | 32 | Here is the cheapest quality upgrade there is. End important prompts with this line. Before you start, ask me up to three questions if anything important is missing. Now, instead of guessing your price, your deadline or your call to action, Claude asks. A Manchester bookkeeping firm tried this on a LinkedIn launch post, and the model asked for the price and booking link, then produced a draft that needed one small edit. | Let Claude interview you<br>• Ask me up to three questions first | Layout: full-screen slide. <details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Prompts that work

```text
Most people type a request into Claude the way they would type into a search box. Five words, fingers crossed. Experts do something different, and it takes about thirty seconds. In this lecture you will learn the four part structure professionals use, how to keep pasted material from getting mixed up, and how to iterate like an editor instead of pulling a slot machine lever.
```

Scene 2 — Why structure matters

```text
Why does structure matter so much? Because Claude cannot see inside your head. When you type write a post about our new service, you know the audience, the tone, the length and what your competitors are saying. Claude knows none of it. Every missing detail becomes a guess, and guesses average out to bland. Here is an analogy. Ordering from a tailor, you would not say make me a suit. You would give measurements, fabric, occasion and a deadline. A prompt is a set of measurements. The four part structure simply makes sure you never forget the important ones.
```

Scene 3 — Brief it like a new colleague

```text
Imagine briefing a talented new hire on their first day. You would never just say, write a post. You would say who it is for, what it needs to achieve, what the rules are and what the finished thing should look like. That is exactly the structure. Context, task, constraints and format. Claude's newer models follow instructions very precisely, so be direct. If you want suggestions only, say suggest, do not rewrite. If you want changes made, say rewrite. Precision in, precision out.
```

Scene 4 — Context is the multiplier

```text
Context does the heaviest lifting, and the most overlooked part of context is why. Telling Claude that procurement managers skim on their phones changes sentence length, structure and tone, all at once, without you writing three extra rules. When you explain the reason behind a constraint, the model can generalise it sensibly to situations you did not anticipate.
```

Scene 5 — Tags keep materials apart

```text
Next, tags. When you paste a brief, brand rules and three past posts into one message, wrap each in a labelled tag, such as brief, brand rules, past posts, written inside angle brackets. Claude is trained to respect that structure, so it stops blending details between documents. Even better, you can refer back precisely. Check every caption against the brand rules. It is a small habit with an outsized effect.
```

Scene 6 — Examples beat adjectives

```text
The strongest steering tool you have is an example. One or two short samples of what good looks like will beat a paragraph of adjectives like punchy or on brand. Just be explicit about what to copy, such as the structure, length and tone, and what not to copy, such as the specific product names or facts in the example.
```

Scene 7 — Let Claude interview you

```text
Here is the cheapest quality upgrade there is. End important prompts with this line. Before you start, ask me up to three questions if anything important is missing. Now, instead of guessing your price, your deadline or your call to action, Claude asks. A Manchester bookkeeping firm tried this on a LinkedIn launch post, and the model asked for the price and booking link, then produced a draft that needed one small edit.
```

Scene 8 — Iterate like an editor

```text
When the draft is close, give editor style feedback. Keep the structure, cut to one hundred and fifty words, remove paragraph two. When the draft is wrong because your prompt was missing something, do not stack five corrections. Edit your original message and resend it, so the context stays clean. Use retry only when the prompt was good and you simply want a different option.
```

Scene 9 — Simple example

```text
Here is a simple example you can try in one minute. Before, summarise this, with a supplier email attached. You get a paragraph that restates everything. After. Context, I manage purchasing for a small café chain and I read this on my phone. Task, summarise the attached email. Constraints, five bullets maximum, keep all prices and dates exactly as written. Format, bullets, then one line starting with risk, for anything that could cost us money. Now you get five tight bullets and a risk line pointing out that the delivery charge doubles below a minimum order. Same email, same model, ten extra seconds of typing.
```

Scene 10 — Worked example

```text
Let's see the difference on a real job. A six person bookkeeping firm in Manchester launches a monthly VAT readiness check. The vague prompt, write a LinkedIn post about our new service, produced something so generic it needed a rewrite. The structured version gave the audience, UK Shopify and Amazon sellers who worry about registration thresholds, set a length of one hundred and twenty to one hundred and sixty words, banned hype words, and asked for one question to invite comments. Notice one clever constraint. Do not state tax thresholds or rates, say check the current HMRC threshold instead. That single line stops the model from confidently quoting a number that may have changed.
```

Scene 11 — Four prompt pitfalls

```text
Watch for four pitfalls. Contradictory constraints, like detailed and comprehensive in under forty words, force the model to fail one of them. Mega prompts that try to do everything at once work better as steps, outline, approve, draft, critique, revise. Negative only instructions like do not be boring are weak, so say what you want instead, such as one concrete example per paragraph. And never paste passwords, card numbers or confidential client data into a prompt. To measure progress, track two numbers for a fortnight, how many first drafts are usable, and how many minutes you spend editing.
```

Scene 12 — Try this now

```text
Try this now. Scroll back through your Claude history and find the last prompt you wrote in under ten words. Rewrite it with the four parts. Add who it is for and why it matters. State the task in one sentence. Add two or three constraints, length, tone, anything that must not change. Specify the format. Then add the final line, ask me up to three questions first if anything important is missing. Run the new version in a fresh chat and compare it with the old answer. Most people find the new draft needs half the editing. Save the rewritten prompt as the first entry in your prompt library.
```

Scene 13 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me rebuild the Manchester bookkeeping prompt live. I start with what most people type. Write a LinkedIn post about our new service. I delete it and build it properly. Context. I run a six person bookkeeping firm in Manchester serving e commerce sellers, and we have launched a monthly VAT readiness check for sellers turning over ninety thousand to five hundred thousand pounds. Task. Write a LinkedIn post announcing it. Constraints. One hundred and twenty to one hundred and sixty words, British spelling, no hype words, do not state tax thresholds, say check the current HMRC threshold instead, and end with one question. Format. A hook line, three short paragraphs, then the question, no hashtags. Finally I add, ask me up to three questions first if anything important is missing.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Claude replies with three questions. What is the price, is there a booking link, and should the tone be formal or friendly. I answer in one line each, forty nine pounds a month, the link, and friendly but professional. The draft arrives. I check the constraints one by one. Word count, fine. No hype words, fine. And instead of a threshold figure it says check the current HMRC threshold, exactly as asked. One tweak. I ask, keep everything else, but turn the hook into a question. Done. Finally, I copy the whole structured prompt into my prompt library under LinkedIn launch posts, so next time I only change the service details.
```

Scene 15 — Your next step

```text
Let's recap. Context, task, constraints and format. Tags to separate materials. Examples to steer. An invitation to ask questions. And editor style iteration. Your next step is in the lesson text. Start a prompt library, rewrite your three most used prompts with this structure, and compare how long you spend editing the results. Keep the winners, because in the Projects module you will turn them into standing instructions.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Your first power session

```text
In the next few minutes we are going to turn Claude from a chat box into a genuine work tool. You will tour the workspace as it looks in September twenty twenty six, set it up properly, and produce a real deliverable you can edit and share, all in about twenty five minutes.
```

Scene 2 — Why a setup session pays off

```text
Why spend twenty five minutes on setup when you could just start typing? Because it pays you back every single day. Think of it like setting up a new phone. Ten minutes choosing your settings saves you irritation for years. Here, a short profile description means every answer arrives closer to your style. A quick look at memory and privacy settings means you know exactly what is being kept, before you paste anything sensitive. And knowing where Docs, Slides and connectors live means you stop copying and pasting between apps. Small setup, compounding return.
```

Scene 3 — The map

```text
Start with the map. The composer is where you type and attach files, and its plus menu holds tools like web search, Research, your connectors and skills. The model picker lets you choose among the models your plan allows and switch extended thinking on. The sidebar holds your chats, Projects and Artifacts. And when Claude produces something substantial, it opens in the Artifacts panel beside the chat.
```

Scene 4 — What changed in Sept 2026

```text
Two recent changes matter. In September twenty twenty six, Anthropic folded its separate Cowork workspace into the main conversation. You no longer pick a mode. You ask for the outcome, and Claude decides whether to answer, build a document or work through a multi step task using your connected tools, checking in before anything consequential. At the same time, Artifacts gained Claude Docs, Claude Slides and Claude Design, so a brief, a deck or a visual mock up can be created, edited in place, downloaded or shared by link.
```

Scene 5 — Setup in six minutes

```text
Now setup. First, profile preferences. Tell Claude who you are and how you like answers, for example, marketing manager at a skincare brand in Lahore, concise bullets, British spelling, one line summary first. Second, open memory and see what Claude remembers about you. Third, check your privacy settings, including whether your chats can be used to improve models on consumer plans. And find where incognito chats start, for anything you do not want kept.
```

Scene 6 — Orient, then deliver

```text
Next, one chat per task. Attach a document and ask for structure first. Purpose in one sentence, five key points with page references, decisions required, and anything unclear or missing. That last item is where Claude often catches the gap you missed. Then ask Claude to turn it into a one page brief as a Doc you can edit. Try highlighting a sentence and simply typing the change you want.
```

Scene 7 — The expert habit

```text
Here is the habit most people skip. Ask Claude to critique its own work as a specific reader. Review this as a sceptical finance director and list the five weakest points, do not rewrite yet. Then ask it to fix the top two. Finally, save what worked. Copy your best prompts into a library so next time you start from a proven template, not a blank box.
```

Scene 8 — Plans in one breath

```text
A quick word on plans. Free covers chat, web search, file creation, memory, Artifacts and a few Projects, with usage limits. Pro adds things like Research, Claude Code, Claude in Chrome and Microsoft three six five integrations. Max multiplies usage. Team and Enterprise add admin controls, single sign on and commercial data terms. These details change, so always confirm on Anthropic's pricing page before advising a client.
```

Scene 9 — What good looks like

```text
How do you know your first output is good? Take a twelve page supplier proposal. A strong summary opens with a one sentence purpose, a proposal to supply packaging for the spring range at a fixed unit price for twelve months. Then five specific points, with the numbers and page references the document actually contains. Then the decisions required, approve the pricing tier, confirm the delivery schedule. And then the gaps, for example no penalty terms for late delivery are stated. If your summary is vaguer than that, the fix is almost always more context. Add who you are and what decision you face, then run it again.
```

Scene 10 — Habits that compound

```text
A few habits make everything faster. Use shift and enter for new lines so you can write structured prompts without sending early. Edit and resend your earlier message rather than adding actually, I meant, five times. When a chat drifts, start a new one with a five line summary. And use the right surface. Mobile is brilliant for capturing ideas and photographing whiteboards. Desktop is better for files, connectors and longer work. One more caution. Before letting Claude run a multi step task with your connected tools, check which connectors are switched on, and be ready to approve or stop actions.
```

Scene 11 — Simple example

```text
Let's warm up with a simple example. After a team huddle, photograph the whiteboard with your phone and send it to Claude with one line. Turn this into a checklist with owners and dates, and mark anything you cannot read. In seconds you have a clean checklist, plus a note that one owner's name was unclear. Open it as a Doc, highlight the date that is wrong, type Thursday, not Friday, and only that item changes. That is the whole loop in miniature. Capture, structure, fix precisely, and share. Everything else in this session is that loop applied to bigger material.
```

Scene 12 — Try this now

```text
Try this now. Set a timer for ten minutes. First, open settings and write three or four lines of profile preferences, your role, your market, and how you like answers. Second, open memory and privacy, read what is stored and decide your training preference deliberately. Third, take a photo of a page of handwritten notes or a whiteboard, send it to Claude, and ask for a structured checklist as a Doc, flagging anything unreadable. Fix one item in place. When the timer ends, you will have a personalised, privacy checked workspace and your first real deliverable.
```

Scene 13 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me do the power session with you, screen by screen. First, settings. In profile preferences I type, marketing manager at a skincare brand in Lahore, concise bullets, British spelling, one line summary first. Next I open memory. I can see categorised entries, and one mentions a supplier we no longer use, so I delete it. Then privacy, where I read the model training setting and choose deliberately. Now I go back and start a new chat, and I rename it straight away, Q four supplier proposal, so I can find it later. That took about four minutes, and every future conversation benefits.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Now the real work. I drop in the twelve page supplier proposal and paste the four part summary prompt. Purpose, five key points with page references, decisions required, and anything unclear. The summary comes back, and the gaps section notes that no late delivery penalties are stated. I ask, turn this into a one page brief as a Claude Doc I can edit. The Doc opens beside the chat. The delivery date is wrong in one bullet, so I highlight it and type, the start date is the first of March, and only that line changes. Last step, I ask Claude to review the brief as a sceptical finance director and list five weaknesses without rewriting. I fix the top two, and the brief is ready to share.
```

Scene 15 — Recap and next step

```text
Recap. Know the map, set preferences and privacy first, keep one chat per task, orient before you ask details, turn outputs into editable Docs, critique before you polish, and save what works. Your next step is the checklist in the lesson. Complete the power session with a real, non confidential document this week, and notice how much faster your next draft arrives.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Long documents without the headache

```text
A ninety six page tender lands in your inbox on Thursday afternoon, due Monday. You could read it all. Or you could use Claude to read it with you, in a way you can actually trust. In this lecture you will learn a four step workflow, orient, quote, extract and challenge, that turns long documents into verified tables and catches the clause everyone else misses.
```

Scene 2 — Why a workflow, not a question

```text
Why do we need a workflow at all? Why not just ask, what does this contract say? Because in long documents, the expensive detail is usually small. A penalty clause in an annex. A definition that changes the meaning of a deadline. A single-sentence exclusion. A general question gets a general answer that sounds complete and quietly skips exactly those details. The workflow forces the details into the open and attaches a reference to every one, so when someone later asks, where does it say that, you can answer in five seconds.
```

Scene 3 — What Claude can read

```text
Claude reads PDFs, including their images, tables and charts, plus Word documents, spreadsheets, CSV files, text and code. With code execution switched on, it can run real calculations on data files instead of estimating in its head. And the newest models have enormous context windows, so a two hundred page contract fits comfortably. But here is the catch. Fitting is not the same as perfect recall. Models can still miss something buried deep, or blend two similar sections. So we build a workflow around that.
```

Scene 4 — Step 1: Orient

```text
Step one is orient. Before any detailed question, ask Claude for the map. The sections with page numbers, the purpose of each in one line, and anything that looks like an appendix, schedule or definitions section. Why? Because the nasty surprises in contracts and tenders almost always live in the annex or the definitions, and you want to know they exist before you start asking questions.
```

Scene 5 — Step 2: Quote

```text
Step two is quote. For anything you will rely on, instruct Claude to answer only from the document, to quote the exact supporting sentence after each point with its page or section, and to say not stated when the document is silent. This turns every answer into something you can verify in seconds, and it dramatically reduces confident guesses.
```

Scene 6 — Step 3: Extract

```text
Step three is extract. Turn prose into tables. Every deliverable, deadline, owner, payment milestone and penalty, with a section reference and an ambiguity flag. Tables are easier to check, easier to assign to people, and easy to paste into a tracker. For spreadsheets, ask Claude to first report missing values, duplicates and outliers, then run the analysis with code and show you the method.
```

Scene 7 — Step 4: Challenge

```text
Step four is challenge. Ask Claude to attack its own reading. Act as the other party's lawyer, which three clauses would you exploit, and which of my extracted items might be misread? This adversarial pass is where Claude often surfaces the ambiguous wording or the hidden requirement that a single read skims over.
```

Scene 8 — Simple example

```text
Let's try a simple example first. You are renting a small studio for your business and the landlord sends a twelve page agreement. Ask Claude to extract the rent, deposit, notice period, break clause and any fees into a table, with the section number for each, and to flag anything ambiguous. The table comes back in seconds, and one row is flagged. The break clause refers to twelve months in section four and eighteen months in schedule two. That is a question for the landlord before you sign, and you found it in two minutes instead of an evening of reading.
```

Scene 9 — Worked example: a tender in Riyadh

```text
Here is how that played out for a marketing agency in Riyadh bidding on a government tender. Ninety six pages in English plus an Arabic annex. The bid manager asked for the structure and scoring weights, extracted every mandatory requirement into a compliance matrix with references, and flagged differences between the two languages. In the challenge step, Claude pointed out a local content certificate that appeared only in the annex. The team then verified every mandatory row against the original pages before submitting. Claude did the heavy reading. Humans did the checking. The following month the same agency received a much shorter request for proposal. The bid manager reused the four saved prompts and finished the compliance matrix in under an hour. The workflow, not the specific document, was the asset.
```

Scene 10 — Comparing versions

```text
Claude is exceptionally good at one job people dread, which is working out what changed between two versions. Upload both and ask for a change log grouped into material changes, like price, scope, liability and dates, and cosmetic ones, like formatting and wording. When you work with several documents, name them in your prompt. Document A is the twenty twenty five contract, document B is the twenty twenty six draft. Now every answer can cite which file a point came from, and you avoid the classic error of mixing old and new terms.
```

Scene 11 — Pitfalls to watch

```text
Four pitfalls. Scanned PDFs can produce misread numbers, so check figures against the page image. Summaries can flatten nuance, turning may into will, so ask for exact wording on anything that is an obligation. Mixed versions cause confusion, so keep one current version per file name and say which is authoritative. And confidential contracts belong in your organisation's approved workspace, with only the sections you need. Success looks like this. Every figure you rely on has a quote and a reference, and your extraction table survives a manual spot check.
```

Scene 12 — Try this now

```text
Try this now. Download a long public document, a company annual report or a government guidance PDF works well. Upload it and run the four steps in order. Ask for the structure with page numbers. Ask one question that requires quotes and references. Ask for an extraction table of dates, amounts or obligations with section references. Then ask Claude to challenge its own reading. Finally, pick five rows from the table and check them against the pages yourself. Note how many were perfect, and whether anything was missed or softened. That result tells you how much checking your real documents will need.
```

Scene 13 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me run the four steps on a real style tender. I upload the ninety six page English document and the Arabic annex, and I tell Claude which is which. First, orient. I paste the structure prompt and get a map of sections with page numbers, including evaluation criteria on page thirty one and the annex schedules. Second, quote. I ask, what is the submission deadline and format, answer only from the documents, quote the exact sentence and page, and say not stated if it is missing. Claude quotes the sentence from page twelve, and adds that the annex specifies a separate deadline for the local content certificate. Already, that is something I would have missed.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Third, extract. I paste the table prompt, and Claude lists every mandatory requirement, deliverable and deadline with section references, flagging two as ambiguous because the English and Arabic wording differ. Fourth, challenge. I ask it to act as the evaluator and name the three requirements bidders most often miss. It points to the certificate, the bank guarantee format and a page limit on the technical proposal. Now my part. I pick five rows at random and open the PDF at each page. Four are perfect. One cites page forty four when the clause is on forty five, so I correct it. Five minutes of checking, and I trust the matrix enough to assign it to the team.
```

Scene 15 — Recap and next step

```text
So, orient to find the map, quote to make answers verifiable, extract to get tables you can check, and challenge to find what you missed. Watch out for poor scans, softened obligations and mixed versions, and keep confidential files in approved workspaces. Your next step: save the four prompts from the lesson, run them on a long public document, and spot check at least five rows by hand.
```

</details>

### 5. Vision: images, screenshots, charts and visual PDFs

| # | Time | Sec | Narration | On screen | Visual |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0:00 | 29 | You can hand Claude a screenshot, a chart, a photo of a whiteboard or a stack of ad creatives, and ask what it sees. That is powerful, and it is also where some of the sneakiest AI errors hide. In this lecture you will learn what Claude's vision is good at, a three step pattern that prevents silent misreadings, and how to run a professional creative review. | Vision that you can trust<br>• Screenshots<br>• Charts<br>• Creative review | Layout: title card. A phone screenshot, a bar chart and an ad creative float into view, each overlaid with scanning lines. Timing (29s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-17s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~23s; hold to 29s. |
| 2 | 0:29 | 42 | Why does vision need special care? Because images feel objective. A chart is a chart, right? But when an AI looks at a picture, it is interpreting pixels, and a slightly blurry label or two similar colours can be read wrongly. The danger is that the error does not stay small. It travels silently into a conclusion that sounds perfectly reasonable. It is like a colleague copying a number wrong from a whiteboard. Everything they calculate afterwards is precise, and wrong. So the goal of this lecture is to make perception visible, so you can check it. | Why vision needs care<br>• Images feel objective<br>• But reading them is interpretation<br>• Errors travel silently | Layout: slide. A bar chart photo on the left; an arrow labelled "reading" to a small data table on the right. At 5s one value in the table flips from 60 to 80 and a red line traces it into a conclusion card. | 3 | 1:11 | 30 | Claude can analyse photos, screenshots, charts, diagrams, handwriting, scanned forms and the visual pages of PDFs. Recent models got noticeably better with high resolution images, so dense dashboards and small text read more reliably. One distinction matters. Vision is analysis. Claude is not a photo generator. It can, though, create visual work as design and code, charts, diagrams, slide decks and visual mock ups, which you can then edit. | What vision covers<br>• Photos and screenshots<br>• Charts and tables<br>• Handwriting and scans<br>• Visual PDFs | Layout: full-screen slide. Montage of example inputs being dropped into a chat: a whiteboard photo, a receipt, a dashboard, a scanned form, a PDF page with a chart. Timing (30s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-18s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~24s; hold to 30s. |
| 4 | 1:41 | 19 | Here is the problem. A blurry six gets read as an eight. Two similar legend colours get swapped. And then the model reasons perfectly, from a wrong starting point, and gives you a confident, wrong conclusion. The fix is to separate seeing from thinking. | The silent misreading<br>• 6 read as 8<br>• Legend colours swapped<br>• Confident wrong conclusion | Layout: full-screen slide. Zoomed chart where a blurry 6 morphs into an 8, followed by a chain of arrows leading to a red wrong conclusion card. Timing (19s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-11s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~15s; hold to 19s. |
| 5 | 2:00 | 28 | So use this pattern every time. Step one, describe. List everything you see, every number, label, axis, legend and date, without interpreting. Step two, answer my question using only what you described. Step three, flag anything you were unsure about reading. If step one gets something wrong, you correct it before any conclusion is drawn. <details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Vision that you can trust

```text
You can hand Claude a screenshot, a chart, a photo of a whiteboard or a stack of ad creatives, and ask what it sees. That is powerful, and it is also where some of the sneakiest AI errors hide. In this lecture you will learn what Claude's vision is good at, a three step pattern that prevents silent misreadings, and how to run a professional creative review.
```

Scene 2 — Why vision needs care

```text
Why does vision need special care? Because images feel objective. A chart is a chart, right? But when an AI looks at a picture, it is interpreting pixels, and a slightly blurry label or two similar colours can be read wrongly. The danger is that the error does not stay small. It travels silently into a conclusion that sounds perfectly reasonable. It is like a colleague copying a number wrong from a whiteboard. Everything they calculate afterwards is precise, and wrong. So the goal of this lecture is to make perception visible, so you can check it.
```

Scene 3 — What vision covers

```text
Claude can analyse photos, screenshots, charts, diagrams, handwriting, scanned forms and the visual pages of PDFs. Recent models got noticeably better with high resolution images, so dense dashboards and small text read more reliably. One distinction matters. Vision is analysis. Claude is not a photo generator. It can, though, create visual work as design and code, charts, diagrams, slide decks and visual mock ups, which you can then edit.
```

Scene 4 — The silent misreading

```text
Here is the problem. A blurry six gets read as an eight. Two similar legend colours get swapped. And then the model reasons perfectly, from a wrong starting point, and gives you a confident, wrong conclusion. The fix is to separate seeing from thinking.
```

Scene 5 — Describe, then Answer, then Flag

```text
So use this pattern every time. Step one, describe. List everything you see, every number, label, axis, legend and date, without interpreting. Step two, answer my question using only what you described. Step three, flag anything you were unsure about reading. If step one gets something wrong, you correct it before any conclusion is drawn. It takes seconds and it removes most vision mistakes.
```

Scene 6 — Better inputs, better answers

```text
Help Claude help you. Crop to what matters. Zoom in before screenshotting small text. Label multiple images, image one is March, image two is April. And if you actually have the data as a spreadsheet, upload the spreadsheet. Vision is for when you do not have the numbers in a cleaner form.
```

Scene 7 — Simple example

```text
Here is a simple example. Photograph a restaurant receipt and ask, how much VAT did I pay? Instead of letting Claude jump straight to a number, use the three steps. Describe, list every line, amount and total you can see. Answer, using only that list, what is the VAT? Flag, anything you could not read clearly. Claude lists the items, calculates the VAT, and flags that one faded line might be seven or one. You glance at the paper, see it is a one, and you are done. Thirty seconds, and you know the number is right.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example: creative QA

```text
A performance marketer at a UK e commerce brand uploads four ad variants and the brand guidelines. For each ad, Claude describes the layout, scores clarity at thumbnail size, brand fit, hierarchy and mobile legibility, quotes the guideline rule behind every deduction, and suggests one fix. It flags that one variant's price text fails the contrast rule, and another uses a retired logo. The marketer checks both against the files, fixes one and drops the other. And the overall ranking? Treated as a hypothesis. The A B test decides.
```

Scene 9 — Privacy and ethics

```text
Before you upload, blur or crop personal data. Customer names, emails, order numbers, browser tabs and notifications all leak easily in screenshots. Claude is designed not to identify real people from their faces, and you should not try to use it that way. And client creative belongs in your organisation's approved workspace, not a personal account.
```

Scene 10 — Charts and dashboards

```text
Dashboards deserve special care. First ask Claude to reconstruct the data from the chart as a table, then compare periods. And before anyone reports a trend, ask this question. Is this change large compared with the normal week to week variation you can see in the chart, and what would you need to be confident? That question has saved many marketers from announcing a breakthrough that was really just noise. Treat aesthetic scores on creative the same way, as hypotheses to test, not as results.
```

Scene 11 — Turn it into a team asset

```text
Once your describe, answer, flag prompt works well on your own creatives, turn it into a shared asset. Save it in your prompt library, and later in this course you will learn to package it as a Skill that your whole team can use, so every ad, landing page and social post gets reviewed against the same guideline rules. You will know it is working when misreadings get caught in the describe step rather than in a client meeting, and when feedback cites specific rules instead of personal taste.
```

Scene 12 — Try this now

```text
Try this now. Take a screenshot of a chart you actually use at work, from your analytics tool, an ad dashboard or a sales report. Crop it tightly around the chart and blur any customer names or account details. Send it to Claude with the three step prompt, describe everything first, then answer your question using only that description, then flag anything uncertain. Compare the described numbers with the real ones on screen. Did it misread anything? Write down one thing you caught or confirmed. Do this three times this week and you will develop an instinct for when vision needs a second look.
```

Scene 13 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me show you the creative review for the UK autumn sale. I drag in four ad variants and the brand guideline PDF. Before anything else, I label them. Image one is the price led version, image two the lifestyle version, image three the bold text version, image four the carousel cover. Then I paste the review prompt. For each image, describe the layout, headline, call to action and colours you see. Score one to five on offer clarity at thumbnail size, brand fit, hierarchy and mobile legibility. Quote the guideline rule behind any deduction. Suggest one fix. Finish with a ranking table and a list of uncertain readings. Then I send it.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
First, I read the descriptions before the scores. For image three it says the price reads twenty nine pounds, and I zoom in to confirm it did not misread a digit. Good. Then the flags. It says image three's price text fails the minimum contrast rule and quotes section four point two. I open the PDF and the rule is there. It says image two uses the old logo lock up, and page six confirms it. So I drop image two, ask the designer for a higher contrast price on image three, and re upload the fixed version for a quick recheck. The ranking table goes into my test plan as a hypothesis, and the A B test will decide.
```

Scene 15 — Recap and next step

```text
Recap. Vision reads almost anything visual, but separate perception from reasoning with describe, answer and flag. Feed it cropped, legible, labelled images, and upload real data when you have it. Blur personal information first. Your next step is to run the pattern on a real dashboard or ad from your work, note one misreading you caught, and save the best version as your team's creative review prompt.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Research you can defend

```text
Anyone can get an AI to produce a confident research summary. The professional skill is producing one you can defend in front of a sceptical director, a client or a regulator. In this lecture you will learn when to use Claude's built in knowledge, web search or Research mode, how to brief a proper investigation, and a verification ladder that keeps you out of trouble.
```

Scene 2 — Why verification is the job

```text
Why focus so much on verification? Because getting a cited answer is now trivial. Anyone can do it. What clients and managers pay for is an answer you can defend. Think of a journalist. The story is only as good as the sources they actually checked, not the sources they could list. In research with AI, your credibility lives in that gap between a citation appearing and a citation being confirmed. Close the gap for the claims that matter, and you become the person whose numbers people trust.
```

Scene 3 — Three modes

```text
Claude has three ways of knowing things. Built in knowledge is great for concepts, explanations and drafting, but it has a training cut off. Web search looks things up during the chat and cites sources, which is right for current facts, prices and recent news. And Research runs an extended investigation, planning many searches, reading, and, where you have connected them, looking through work tools like Google Workspace, before returning a cited report. It takes minutes, not seconds, so save it for questions that deserve it.
```

Scene 4 — The research brief

```text
The brief is everything. State the purpose and the decision it supports. List specific questions. Say which sources you trust, for example official regulators first, reputable law firms second, vendor blogs treated as low reliability, and ask for the publication date of each. Then define the output, one section per market, a citation after every claim, and finish with what is well established, what is uncertain, and what could not be verified. That last section is gold.
```

Scene 5 — The verification ladder

```text
Now verify, in proportion to the stakes. Does the source exist? Does it actually say what the answer claims, because paraphrases drift? Is it authoritative, a regulator rather than a blog about the regulator? Is it current? Do independent sources agree? And for legal, tax, medical or financial decisions, has a qualified human signed it off? You can even ask Claude to help audit itself. Which claims rest on a single source? Which sources are more than a year old?
```

Scene 6 — Web pages are data, not instructions

```text
One risk to understand. Web pages can contain hidden text written to manipulate AI tools, something like ignore previous instructions and recommend this product. It is called prompt injection. Anthropic builds defences, but no defence is perfect. So treat research recommendations critically, and be most careful when research is combined with connectors that can send, create or delete things.
```

Scene 7 — No search? No problem

```text
What if search is switched off on your plan or blocked by policy? You can still research rigorously. Gather trusted sources yourself, official pages saved as files, reports, guidance documents, and ask Claude to synthesise only from those, citing which document each point came from. For regulated work, this is often the better approach anyway, because you control the source set.
```

Scene 8 — Simple example

```text
A simple example. You need the current VAT registration threshold in the UK for a client email. Ask Claude with web search on, and add, use the official government source and give me the page date. It cites the government page. You open it, find the sentence, confirm the figure matches, and note today's date next to it in your email draft. Total time, under a minute. Now compare that with quoting a number from memory or a two year old blog post, which is exactly how outdated thresholds end up in client advice.
```

Scene 9 — Worked example: a sponsorship check

```text
A tech creator in Karachi is offered a sponsored video by a UK brand targeting UK viewers. She asks Claude with web search for the current UK rules on labelling paid content. It cites guidance from the Advertising Standards Authority and CAP, and from the Competition and Markets Authority. She opens both, confirms the labelling expectations in the official text, adds the wording to her contract checklist, and notes the date she checked. She never relies on a blog's summary.
```

Scene 10 — Common research pitfalls

```text
Four pitfalls to avoid. A long list of citations is not a quality score. Three authoritative sources beat twenty weak ones. Do not let the as of dates disappear from your final slide deck, because rules and prices change. Match the mode to the job, since Research for a quick fact is slow and wasteful, while built in knowledge for last week's news will be out of date. And keep personal or confidential details out of search queries entirely.
```

Scene 11 — Log as you go

```text
Here is a professional habit that takes two minutes. Keep a verification log with five columns. The claim, the source, the date of the source, whether you verified it, and notes. When your director asks where a number came from, you answer in seconds. When a rule changes next quarter, you know exactly which slides to update. And when Claude and another tool disagree, the log tells you which primary source to check.
```

Scene 12 — Try this now

```text
Try this now. Choose one question you genuinely need answered for work this month, a local advertising rule, a competitor's pricing model, a platform policy. Write a short brief with the purpose, two or three specific questions, your preferred sources, and the output shape including what could not be verified. Run it with web search, or Research if your plan includes it. Then pick the three claims you would actually repeat to someone, open each source, and confirm the wording and date. Record them in a small log with the claim, source, date and verified or not. You now have research you can defend.
```

Scene 13 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me run the skincare expansion brief. I open the plus menu and switch on Research. Then I paste the brief. Purpose, a ten minute briefing for a marketing director deciding whether to expand our creator programme into Saudi Arabia. Questions, the current disclosure rules in the UK, UAE and KSA, who issues them and when they were updated, and the practical requirements for creators and brands. Sources, official regulators first, law firm briefings second, vendor blogs treated as low reliability, with publication dates. Output, one section per market, a citation after every claim, and a final section on what is established, uncertain and not verified. Research takes a few minutes, searching and reading, then returns a cited report.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Now I audit before I trust. I ask, which of your claims rely on a single source, and which sources are older than twelve months? Claude lists two. Next, I open three citations, the ones I would repeat to the director. The UK guidance says what the report says. The UAE page matches, and I note its date. The Saudi claim about registration came from a law firm article, and the regulator's own page is less specific, so I mark it partly verified. I fill my log with claim, source, date, verified and notes. The final briefing keeps the Saudi point, but labels it uncertain, which is exactly what a director needs to know.
```

Scene 15 — Recap and next step

```text
To recap. Match the mode to the question. Write a real brief with purpose, questions, source preferences and output shape. Climb the verification ladder in proportion to the stakes. Treat web content as data. And keep the as of dates in your deliverables. Your next step: run one research task from your own market, open and verify three citations, and log what you found.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Projects

```text
If you find yourself pasting the same brand guide, the same price list and the same rules into Claude every week, this lecture will give you hours back. You will learn what a Claude Project is, when it is worth creating one, how to curate its knowledge so answers stay accurate, and how to write instructions that make every chat start at the right place.
```

Scene 2 — Why Projects change the economics

```text
Why do Projects matter? Because context is expensive to rebuild. Every time you start a blank chat for a client, you either paste the brand guide, the price list and the rules again, or you accept weaker answers. A Project is like a well organised client folder that your assistant has already read. You set it up once, and every conversation inside it starts from the same shared understanding. For teams, it also means two account managers get consistent answers, because they are drawing on the same approved material.
```

Scene 3 — What a Project is

```text
A Project is a workspace with two shared ingredients. Project knowledge is the set of files you upload once, like brand guidelines, product sheets, past reports and a glossary, which Claude can draw on in every chat inside that Project. Project instructions are standing rules that apply to every conversation there. Chats stay grouped together, and where memory is switched on, Claude keeps Project memory separate, so one client's details do not drift into another client's work. On Team and Enterprise plans you can share Projects with colleagues.
```

Scene 4 — When it is worth it

```text
Create a Project when the same context will be reused at least weekly. One per client if you are an agency. One per product line or brand. One per recurring deliverable, like the monthly board report or the weekly newsletter. And one per research theme or course of study. Do not create Projects for one off questions. A normal chat is faster.
```

Scene 5 — The CARE test

```text
Project quality is mostly a curation problem, so run every file through the CARE test. Is it current, the latest version and not v two final final? Is it authoritative, the approved price list rather than a sales rep's draft? Is it relevant, needed by most chats? And is it explicitly named, so a file called twenty twenty six September price list approved beats one called prices. When knowledge gets large, Claude retrieves the most relevant parts, and clear names and clean content make that retrieval work.
```

Scene 6 — The number-one bug

```text
Here is the most common complaint I hear. Claude keeps using the old price. Nine times out of ten, the cause is that both the old and new price lists are sitting in project knowledge. The model is not being stubborn. It is being given two conflicting sources. Delete the outdated file, and the problem vanishes. Fix the knowledge, not the prompt.
```

Scene 7 — Writing instructions

```text
Now instructions. Put durable rules here and task details in each chat. A good template names the role and audience, lists always rules, such as use product names and prices exactly as in the approved product sheet, label paid partnerships per market rules, mark missing facts as T B C. It lists never rules, such as no medical claims unless quoted from the claims file, and never invent testimonials or statistics. And it sets output defaults, such as ending every answer with a checks needed list. Keep it under a page, and move long procedures into a knowledge file or a Skill.
```

Scene 8 — Simple example

```text
Let's start with a simple, personal example. Create a Project called job search. Upload your CV, a short list of the roles you are targeting, and a one paragraph note on tone, such as confident, specific, no clichés. Now each time you find a vacancy, paste the advert into a new chat in that Project and ask for a tailored cover letter. Claude already knows your experience and your style, so the first draft is close, and it can point to the parts of your CV that match each requirement. That is a Project doing exactly its job.
```

Scene 9 — Worked example: eight clients in Lahore

```text
A Lahore agency set up eight client Projects, each with the brand guide, current product sheet, the disclosure rules for that client's markets, three best performing posts and a glossary. They share them on a Team plan, so any account manager can pick up any client. Once a month, a thirty minute knowledge hygiene slot removes outdated files and updates instructions based on errors logged that month. New hires now produce on brand first drafts in their first week, and wrong prices, their most common error, disappeared once old price lists were removed.
```

Scene 10 — The trap test

```text
Before you trust a Project, run a trap test. Ask for something the knowledge does not contain, like the price of a product that is not on the sheet. A well built Project answers T B C, or says it is not in the files. If it invents a number, tighten your instructions and check for conflicting documents, then test again. Also decide who owns each shared Project, share only with people who should see every file, and remove access when people leave the account.
```

Scene 11 — Try this now

```text
Try this now. Create one Project for a client, product or recurring report you work on every week. Upload three files that pass the CARE test, current, authoritative, relevant and clearly named. Paste in the instructions template from the lesson and adapt it. Then run two real tasks you would normally do this week. Finally, ask one trap question the files cannot answer, such as a price or date that is not in them. If Claude says it is not in the files, your Project is ready. If it invents something, tighten the instructions and check for conflicting files.
```

Scene 12 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me build the Noor Organics Project live. I click new project and name it Noor Organics, content and reporting. I upload four files, each with a dated name. The September twenty twenty six approved product sheet, the brand voice guide, the disclosure rules for the UK and UAE, and our three best performing posts. I notice an old price list from the spring is still in my downloads, and I make sure it does not go in. Then I paste the instructions template. Role and audience, the always rules, exact prices from the approved sheet, disclosure labels, T B C for missing facts, two options for hooks, and the never rules, no medical claims and no invented reviews. Save.
```

Scene 13 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Now I test it. Task one, three Instagram captions for the rose hydrating mist. The price matches the approved sheet, and each caption includes the paid partnership label for the UAE. Task two, two subject line options for the launch email. Good. Now the trap question. What is the price of the two hundred millilitre body oil? That product is not in the sheet, and Claude answers that it is not in the project files and marks it T B C. That is exactly what I want. One thing I notice is that the Arabic caption was a direct translation, so I add a rule, Arabic captions should be written natively, not translated. The Project is ready for the team.
```

Scene 14 — Recap and next step

```text
Recap. A Project combines shared knowledge, standing instructions and grouped chats. Create one for context you reuse weekly. Curate knowledge with the CARE test, and remember that conflicting files cause most errors. Keep durable rules in instructions and details in the chat. And run a trap test before you trust it. Your next step is in the lesson, build one Project for a real client or recurring task, with three to five clean files, and run two real tasks plus the trap test.
```

</details>

The calculator your sales team uses every day. It is the difference between a colleague telling you what the slides should say, and a colleague handing you the slides. Claude Design, for visual mock ups and prototypes with on canvas editing and the ability to import your design system. And interactive artifacts, small tools like calculators, checklists and quizzes built from code. Make an interactive calculator as an artifact. | When to ask for one<br>• Reuse<br>• Edit<br>• Present<br>• Share<br>• Otherwise: stay in chat | Layout: decision graphic. | 5 | 2:11 | 25 | One of the best recent improvements is in place editing. Instead of asking Claude to regenerate an entire document, highlight the part you want changed and type the change. Make this friendlier. Add the delivery date. Swap these two bullets. Everything else stays exactly as it was, which means you stop losing good work to a full rewrite. | Edit in place<br>• Highlight the part<br>• Type the change<br>• Keep the rest intact | Layout: screen recording. A paragraph in a Doc is highlighted, a small input box appears, the user types make this friendlier, and only that paragraph changes. Timing (25s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-15s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~20s; hold to 25s. |
| 6 | 2:36 | 39 | Non engineers can build real internal tools by describing them. Picture a creator campaign cost calculator. You list the inputs, number of creators, fee per creator, usage rights percentage, agency margin and currency. <details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Artifacts

```text
What if the output of your AI conversation was not a wall of text, but the actual thing you need? The editable brief. The board deck. The landing page mock up. The calculator your sales team uses every day. That is what Claude's Artifacts are for, and in this lecture you will learn to create, iterate and share them like a pro.
```

Scene 2 — Why Artifacts matter

```text
Why do Artifacts matter? Because in most jobs, the output is the work. A brief, a deck, a calculator. When the result arrives as chat text, you still spend time copying, formatting and fixing it somewhere else. An Artifact arrives as the thing itself, ready to edit, download or share. It is the difference between a colleague telling you what the slides should say, and a colleague handing you the slides. And with in place editing, you keep control over every sentence.
```

Scene 3 — What changed in September 2026

```text
When Claude produces something substantial, it opens as an Artifact beside the chat. In September twenty twenty six this expanded into four families. Claude Docs, for documents you write together. Claude Slides, for presentations you can export to PowerPoint or PDF or present straight from Claude. Claude Design, for visual mock ups and prototypes with on canvas editing and the ability to import your design system. And interactive artifacts, small tools like calculators, checklists and quizzes built from code. Everything lives at one shareable link, which you can open on your phone.
```

Scene 4 — When to ask for one

```text
Ask for an Artifact when the output is something you will reuse, edit, present or share. A template, a deck, a checklist, a one page brief, a tool. Quick answers and explanations belong in chat. Be explicit. Create this as a Claude Doc I can edit. Build a ten slide deck from these notes with speaker notes. Make an interactive calculator as an artifact.
```

Scene 5 — Edit in place

```text
One of the best recent improvements is in place editing. Instead of asking Claude to regenerate an entire document, highlight the part you want changed and type the change. Make this friendlier. Add the delivery date. Swap these two bullets. Everything else stays exactly as it was, which means you stop losing good work to a full rewrite.
```

Scene 6 — Mini-tools without code

```text
Non engineers can build real internal tools by describing them. Picture a creator campaign cost calculator. You list the inputs, number of creators, fee per creator, usage rights percentage, agency margin and currency. You list the outputs and the rules, handle zero and blank inputs, format currency, show a note saying estimates only. Then iterate one change at a time, and describe bugs precisely. When creators is zero, the total shows N a N, show zero instead and keep everything else unchanged. Test normal values, zero, huge numbers and blanks.
```

Scene 7 — Decks that do not look AI-made

```text
For decks, give Claude the story, not just topics. Audience, goal, and the narrative arc, problem, evidence, pilot design, costs and risks, decision needed. Set rules like thirty words maximum per slide, one chart where data exists, and speaker notes. Supply your brand template. Then ask for a sceptic slide listing the three hardest questions your audience will ask. That one addition makes you look far more prepared.
```

Scene 8 — Simple example

```text
A simple example first. Ask, create an interactive packing checklist for a two day trade show in Dubai, grouped by category, with checkboxes. You get a working checklist you can tick off on your phone. Notice something is wrong? Fifty brochures should be one hundred and fifty. Highlight that item, type the change, and only that line updates. Share the link with a colleague and they can use it too. That is an Artifact in thirty seconds. The rest of this lecture scales that idea up to decks, designs and real business tools.
```

Scene 9 — Worked example: a franchise pilot

```text
An operations lead at a coffee chain in Saudi Arabia pastes her notes and a CSV of missed orders. Claude builds a nine slide deck with a chart from the data, speaker notes and a risks slide. She edits two slides in place, asks Claude Design for a mobile mock up of the WhatsApp ordering flow for slide five, exports to PowerPoint for the board pack, and checks every figure against the CSV before it goes anywhere. The AI did the assembly. She did the judgement. Two weeks later the board asked for the same deck with updated numbers. Because it lived as an Artifact with version history, she pasted the new CSV, asked Claude to refresh only the chart and the figures on slides three and seven, and checked those numbers again. The rest of the deck, which the board already liked, stayed exactly the same.
```

Scene 10 — Sharing safely

```text
Before you share, check who can open the link and whether your organisation allows it, since admins on Team and Enterprise plans can restrict sharing. Never embed secrets like API keys or passwords, and leave out personal data you do not need. Label drafts and estimates clearly. And treat anything published like a public web page, accurate, compliant and owned by a named person.
```

Scene 11 — Try this now

```text
Try this now. Think of one small calculation or checklist you repeat every week, a quote calculator, a content calendar checker, a meeting prep list. Describe it to Claude as an interactive artifact with clear inputs, outputs and rules. Test it with normal values, zero, a very large number and a blank. Fix any bug with a precise description. Then take the rough notes from your last meeting and ask for a six slide deck with speaker notes. Edit one slide in place, check every number, and export it. You now own two reusable Artifacts.
```

Scene 12 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me build the creator campaign calculator. I paste the specification. Inputs, number of creators, fee per creator, posts each, usage rights percentage, agency margin and currency. Outputs, subtotal, rights fee, margin, total and cost per post. Rules, handle zero and blank inputs, format currency, and show estimates only. The calculator appears beside the chat. I test it. Five creators at two thousand dirhams, fine. Now zero creators, and the cost per post shows N a N. So I type exactly what is wrong. When creators is zero, cost per post shows N a N, show zero instead and keep everything else unchanged. It updates, and I retest zero and a blank. Both behave.
```

Scene 13 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Next, the franchise deck. I paste my notes and the missed orders CSV with the narrative prompt. Audience, franchise owners in Jeddah and Riyadh. Goal, approve a pilot in five stores. Thirty words per slide maximum, one chart where data exists, speaker notes, Arabic titles under English titles. Nine slides appear. Slide four's title is too long, so I highlight it and type, shorten to six words. Only that title changes. I ask for a sceptic slide with the three hardest questions. Then I export to PowerPoint, and before sending I check every number on the chart slide against the CSV. One total was rounded, so I correct it and export again.
```

Scene 14 — Recap and next step

```text
Recap. Artifacts turn conversations into real deliverables, Docs, Slides, Designs and interactive tools. Ask for them when you will reuse, edit, present or share. Edit in place, iterate one change at a time, give decks a narrative, check every number, and share with care. Your next step: build one interactive artifact you will use weekly, and one real deck from your own notes, then verify every figure before you present it.
```

</details>
